Like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about how other parts of your body look, beyond just the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I suppose everyone has little things they don’t love, especially at my age, but it isn’t like that for me.\n\nIt’s the wrist. That scar just catches my eye, and I know it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hands right there on the table—oh, and bridge, you know, people really do look at your hands because of the cards—and I keep feeling like their eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I’ve had arguments with family or anything, but I’ve pulled back from some things. Choir, for instance—I’ve skipped a few times, and I was supposed to help with a theater volunteer thing, but I didn’t go because I got so caught up trying to figure out what sleeve would cover my wrist, or whether people would notice.\n\nAnd my bookkeeping work too, actually. I’ve been late turning things in, which isn’t like me. I sit there meaning to work, but then I’m fussing with my wrist or thinking about whether it’ll show when I go out later. It sounds silly when I say it, but it takes up more time than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to avoid any important events or activities because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes… not big family things or anything like that, but I have skipped choir a few times, and there was a theater volunteer thing I was meant to help with that I didn’t go to. I just got so caught up with what I could wear so my wrist wouldn’t show, and then I felt embarrassed and stayed home.\n\nAnd even with my bookkeeping, I’ve been late turning things in, which really bothers me because I’m usually careful about that. I’ll mean to sit down and finish it, and then I’m thinking about my sleeve or whether the scar is visible… it’s ridiculous, but it can eat up the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to focus on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am, though I hate admitting that because I’ve always been very reliable with my bookkeeping. I’ll sit down to do the accounts, and then I notice my sleeve has ridden up, or I start thinking about whether the scar will show later if I go out, and suddenly I’ve lost all this time.\n\nI’ve turned things in late because of it, which isn’t like me. And it’s not just work—I’ve missed choir a few times, and that theater volunteer commitment, because I got so caught up trying to make sure my wrist wouldn’t be noticed. I know there are worse problems to have, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more about the way it looks to you or about what you think others might think when they see it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but… more what I imagine other people are seeing, maybe. I know, in my sensible mind, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as awful or noticeable as it seems to me. But then I’ll be at church, or playing bridge, and I just get this feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like that’s the first thing they notice about me now, not my face or what I’m saying or anything. And I know people are probably busy thinking about their own things—at bridge half the time everyone’s worried about the bidding anyway—but I still feel like they’re looking.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, is it more the way it looks that bothers you, or is it what you think it means about you to other people?\n\nPatient: It’s… I suppose it’s the way it looks, but mostly what I imagine it says to other people when they see it. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—my sister would probably tell me nobody is studying my wrist at church, you know, people are looking at the hymn book or chatting about who brought what to coffee hour.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not that I’m there, not what I’m saying—just that. It makes me feel very exposed, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people seeing your scar, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you think it will lead people to judge you as a person?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more that it makes me look… noticeable in the wrong way. Not attractive or unattractive exactly, though I don’t like the look of it at all. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I start wondering what they’re thinking, but it begins with how it looks, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about what people think of you if the scar on your wrist were to disappear?\n\nPatient: If it disappeared? I think… I think I wouldn’t be worrying in the same way, no. I mean, I know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or at bridge I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get all distracted—especially at bridge, which is silly because I should be counting cards, not worrying about my wrist. So if it weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t have that same thought in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about your appearance at all if you were completely alone and nobody could see you?\n\nPatient: If I were completely alone… I think it wouldn’t be as strong, maybe. It’s mostly when I imagine other people seeing it. Like at church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI know, logically, the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. My sister would say, “Nobody’s looking that closely,” and she’s probably right. But when I’m around people, it just feels very obvious. If no one could see me at all, I suppose I might not worry about it in quite the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say “satisfied,” exactly. I mean, at my age you get used to things changing, don’t you? I try not to fuss too much about it. My sister is always saying we earned our wrinkles, which is easy for her to say because she’s always had nicer skin than me—well, not nicer, just different, I suppose.

But aside from the scar… I don’t know. I don’t spend a great deal of time thinking about the rest of me, not like that. It’s really the wrist that seems to pull my attention.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it probably isn’t as awful as it looks to me, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. When I’m at church, or playing bridge with the ladies, I feel like everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I know people are probably just looking at their cards, you know, or the hymn book, but it just… it feels so obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling that people are staring at your scar made it difficult for you to participate in or enjoy activities like playing bridge or attending church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I still go sometimes, but I’ve missed choir a few times, and I’ve backed out of helping with the theater group too. I’ll be getting ready and then I start fussing with my sleeve or a bracelet, trying to figure out if my wrist shows, and before I know it I’m late or I just don’t go.\n\nEven with my bookkeeping work, which is silly because that’s mostly just me at a desk, I’ve been late turning things in because I get so wrapped up in making sure it won’t be noticed. It’s embarrassing to say, really.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about your scar cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s there most days, I’d say. Not always terrible, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me—my daughter would probably tell me I’m making too much of it, she’s very practical that way—but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I can’t really relax, because I’m thinking about where my hand is, or whether my sleeve has slipped up, instead of paying attention to the sermon or the cards. So… distressing, yes. More than I think it ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of the scar itself that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others or the attention it draws?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, in a way. The scar itself bothers me when I look at it—though I do know, somewhere in my head, it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me.\n\nBut then when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to my wrist. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And of course, people are probably just looking at the cards or their bulletin… Mrs. Hensley always squints at everything anyway, even when she’s just reading the announcements. But I can’t shake the feeling that they’ve seen it, and that’s what they’re thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is disproportionate to how noticeable it actually is to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t nearly as noticeable to other people as it is to me. I mean, people at church have plenty else to look at—the bulletin, the choir, who came in late, all of that.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I just feel like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, even if I know that may not be true.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks to you or about what others might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with how it looks to me, because when I see it, it just seems so obvious. I do know, rationally, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying about other people seeing it. At church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I’m not really thinking about anything else, even if, honestly, they’re probably looking at the hymnals or their cards.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more related to its appearance or to the idea that others might judge you because of it?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more the appearance, at first. When I look at my wrist, the scar just seems so… there. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, I do know that.\n\nBut then, when I’m at church or bridge—well, bridge especially, because your hands are right out there on the table—I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So then I worry they’re judging it, or judging me, even if they’re probably just looking at their cards or the bulletin.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance limited to your wrist with the scar, or do you worry about how other parts of your body look as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the wrist, the scar. I don’t really sit around worrying about, you know, my nose or my weight or things like that—not in the same way.\n\nIt’s just that scar feels so noticeable to me. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks in my own head, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, especially with my hands out on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about how other parts of your body look, beyond just the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I suppose everyone has little things they don’t love, especially at my age, but it isn’t like that for me.\n\nIt’s the wrist. That scar just catches my eye, and I know it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hands right there on the table—oh, and bridge, you know, people really do look at your hands because of the cards—and I keep feeling like their eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I’ve had arguments with family or anything, but I’ve pulled back from some things. Choir, for instance—I’ve skipped a few times, and I was supposed to help with a theater volunteer thing, but I didn’t go because I got so caught up trying to figure out what sleeve would cover my wrist, or whether people would notice.\n\nAnd my bookkeeping work too, actually. I’ve been late turning things in, which isn’t like me. I sit there meaning to work, but then I’m fussing with my wrist or thinking about whether it’ll show when I go out later. It sounds silly when I say it, but it takes up more time than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to avoid any important events or activities because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes… not big family things or anything like that, but I have skipped choir a few times, and there was a theater volunteer thing I was meant to help with that I didn’t go to. I just got so caught up with what I could wear so my wrist wouldn’t show, and then I felt embarrassed and stayed home.\n\nAnd even with my bookkeeping, I’ve been late turning things in, which really bothers me because I’m usually careful about that. I’ll mean to sit down and finish it, and then I’m thinking about my sleeve or whether the scar is visible… it’s ridiculous, but it can eat up the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to focus on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am, though I hate admitting that because I’ve always been very reliable with my bookkeeping. I’ll sit down to do the accounts, and then I notice my sleeve has ridden up, or I start thinking about whether the scar will show later if I go out, and suddenly I’ve lost all this time.\n\nI’ve turned things in late because of it, which isn’t like me. And it’s not just work—I’ve missed choir a few times, and that theater volunteer commitment, because I got so caught up trying to make sure my wrist wouldn’t be noticed. I know there are worse problems to have, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more about the way it looks to you or about what you think others might think when they see it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but… more what I imagine other people are seeing, maybe. I know, in my sensible mind, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as awful or noticeable as it seems to me. But then I’ll be at church, or playing bridge, and I just get this feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like that’s the first thing they notice about me now, not my face or what I’m saying or anything. And I know people are probably busy thinking about their own things—at bridge half the time everyone’s worried about the bidding anyway—but I still feel like they’re looking.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, is it more the way it looks that bothers you, or is it what you think it means about you to other people?\n\nPatient: It’s… I suppose it’s the way it looks, but mostly what I imagine it says to other people when they see it. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—my sister would probably tell me nobody is studying my wrist at church, you know, people are looking at the hymn book or chatting about who brought what to coffee hour.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not that I’m there, not what I’m saying—just that. It makes me feel very exposed, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people seeing your scar, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you think it will lead people to judge you as a person?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more that it makes me look… noticeable in the wrong way. Not attractive or unattractive exactly, though I don’t like the look of it at all. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I start wondering what they’re thinking, but it begins with how it looks, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about what people think of you if the scar on your wrist were to disappear?\n\nPatient: If it disappeared? I think… I think I wouldn’t be worrying in the same way, no. I mean, I know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or at bridge I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get all distracted—especially at bridge, which is silly because I should be counting cards, not worrying about my wrist. So if it weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t have that same thought in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about your appearance at all if you were completely alone and nobody could see you?\n\nPatient: If I were completely alone… I think it wouldn’t be as strong, maybe. It’s mostly when I imagine other people seeing it. Like at church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI know, logically, the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. My sister would say, “Nobody’s looking that closely,” and she’s probably right. But when I’m around people, it just feels very obvious. If no one could see me at all, I suppose I might not worry about it in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally satisfied with your body image, aside from the concern about the scar?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “satisfied,” exactly. I mean, at my age you get used to things changing, don’t you? I try not to fuss too much about it. My sister is always saying we earned our wrinkles, which is easy for her to say because she’s always had nicer skin than me—well, not nicer, just different, I suppose.\n\nBut aside from the scar… I don’t know. I don’t spend a great deal of time thinking about the rest of me, not like that. It’s really the wrist that seems to pull my attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So if it weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t have that same thought in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about your appearance at all if you were completely alone and nobody could see you?\n\nPatient: If I were completely alone… I think it wouldn’t be as strong, maybe. It’s mostly when I imagine other people seeing it. Like at church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI know, logically, the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. My sister would say, “Nobody’s looking that closely,” and she’s probably right. But when I’m around people, it just feels very obvious. If no one could see me at all, I suppose I might not worry about it in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally satisfied with your body image, aside from the concern about the scar?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “satisfied,” exactly. I don’t spend a great deal of time thinking about the rest of me, not like that. Less as a person… I don’t know if I’d put it quite that way. I don’t think people at church are cruel, really—Mrs. Hanley still saves me a seat sometimes, unless her cousin comes, and then the whole pew arrangement changes, you know how that is.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it probably isn’t as awful as it looks to me, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it. When I’m at church, or playing bridge with the ladies, I feel like everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I know people are probably just looking at their cards, you know, or the hymn book, but it just… it feels so obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling that people are staring at your scar made it difficult for you to participate in or enjoy activities like playing bridge or attending church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I still go sometimes, but I’ve missed choir a few times, and I’ve backed out of helping with the theater group too. I’ll be getting ready and then I start fussing with my sleeve or a bracelet, trying to figure out if my wrist shows, and before I know it I’m late or I just don’t go.\n\nEven with my bookkeeping work, which is silly because that’s mostly just me at a desk, I’ve been late turning things in because I get so wrapped up in making sure it won’t be noticed. It’s embarrassing to say, really.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about your scar cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s there most days, I’d say. Not always terrible, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it seems to me—my daughter would probably tell me I’m making too much of it, she’s very practical that way—but when I’m at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I can’t really relax, because I’m thinking about where my hand is, or whether my sleeve has slipped up, instead of paying attention to the sermon or the cards. So… distressing, yes. More than I think it ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of the scar itself that bothers you, or is it more about what you think it might mean to others or the attention it draws?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, in a way. The scar itself bothers me when I look at it—though I do know, somewhere in my head, it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me.\n\nBut then when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I keep feeling like people’s eyes go right to my wrist. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And of course, people are probably just looking at the cards or their bulletin… Mrs. Hensley always squints at everything anyway, even when she’s just reading the announcements. But I can’t shake the feeling that they’ve seen it, and that’s what they’re thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is disproportionate to how noticeable it actually is to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, if I’m being honest. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t nearly as noticeable to other people as it is to me. I mean, people at church have plenty else to look at—the bulletin, the choir, who came in late, all of that.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I just feel like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, even if I know that may not be true.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks to you or about what others might think of you because of it?\n\nPatient: I think… it starts with how it looks to me, because when I see it, it just seems so obvious. I do know, rationally, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying about other people seeing it. At church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I’m not really thinking about anything else, even if, honestly, they’re probably looking at the hymnals or their cards.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more related to its appearance or to the idea that others might judge you because of it?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more the appearance, at first. When I look at my wrist, the scar just seems so… there. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, I do know that.\n\nBut then, when I’m at church or bridge—well, bridge especially, because your hands are right out there on the table—I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So then I worry they’re judging it, or judging me, even if they’re probably just looking at their cards or the bulletin.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance limited to your wrist with the scar, or do you worry about how other parts of your body look as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the wrist, the scar. I don’t really sit around worrying about, you know, my nose or my weight or things like that—not in the same way.\n\nIt’s just that scar feels so noticeable to me. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks in my own head, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, especially with my hands out on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about how other parts of your body look, beyond just the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I suppose everyone has little things they don’t love, especially at my age, but it isn’t like that for me.\n\nIt’s the wrist. That scar just catches my eye, and I know it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hands right there on the table—oh, and bridge, you know, people really do look at your hands because of the cards—and I keep feeling like their eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I’ve had arguments with family or anything, but I’ve pulled back from some things. Choir, for instance—I’ve skipped a few times, and I was supposed to help with a theater volunteer thing, but I didn’t go because I got so caught up trying to figure out what sleeve would cover my wrist, or whether people would notice.\n\nAnd my bookkeeping work too, actually. I’ve been late turning things in, which isn’t like me. I sit there meaning to work, but then I’m fussing with my wrist or thinking about whether it’ll show when I go out later. It sounds silly when I say it, but it takes up more time than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to avoid any important events or activities because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes… not big family things or anything like that, but I have skipped choir a few times, and there was a theater volunteer thing I was meant to help with that I didn’t go to. I just got so caught up with what I could wear so my wrist wouldn’t show, and then I felt embarrassed and stayed home.\n\nAnd even with my bookkeeping, I’ve been late turning things in, which really bothers me because I’m usually careful about that. I’ll mean to sit down and finish it, and then I’m thinking about my sleeve or whether the scar is visible… it’s ridiculous, but it can eat up the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to focus on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am, though I hate admitting that because I’ve always been very reliable with my bookkeeping. I’ll sit down to do the accounts, and then I notice my sleeve has ridden up, or I start thinking about whether the scar will show later if I go out, and suddenly I’ve lost all this time.\n\nI’ve turned things in late because of it, which isn’t like me. And it’s not just work—I’ve missed choir a few times, and that theater volunteer commitment, because I got so caught up trying to make sure my wrist wouldn’t be noticed. I know there are worse problems to have, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more about the way it looks to you or about what you think others might think when they see it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but… more what I imagine other people are seeing, maybe. I know, in my sensible mind, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as awful or noticeable as it seems to me. But then I’ll be at church, or playing bridge, and I just get this feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like that’s the first thing they notice about me now, not my face or what I’m saying or anything. And I know people are probably busy thinking about their own things—at bridge half the time everyone’s worried about the bidding anyway—but I still feel like they’re looking.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, is it more the way it looks that bothers you, or is it what you think it means about you to other people?\n\nPatient: It’s… I suppose it’s the way it looks, but mostly what I imagine it says to other people when they see it. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—my sister would probably tell me nobody is studying my wrist at church, you know, people are looking at the hymn book or chatting about who brought what to coffee hour.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not that I’m there, not what I’m saying—just that. It makes me feel very exposed, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people seeing your scar, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you think it will lead people to judge you as a person?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more that it makes me look… noticeable in the wrong way. Not attractive or unattractive exactly, though I don’t like the look of it at all. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I start wondering what they’re thinking, but it begins with how it looks, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about what people think of you if the scar on your wrist were to disappear?\n\nPatient: If it disappeared? I think… I think I wouldn’t be worrying in the same way, no. So if it weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t have that same thought in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about your appearance at all if you were completely alone and nobody could see you?\n\nPatient: If I were completely alone… I think it wouldn’t be as strong, maybe. It’s mostly when I imagine other people seeing it. Like at church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI know, logically, the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. My sister would say, “Nobody’s looking that closely,” and she’s probably right. But when I’m around people, it just feels very obvious. If no one could see me at all, I suppose I might not worry about it in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally satisfied with your body image, aside from the concern about the scar?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “satisfied,” exactly. I don’t really sit around worrying about, you know, my nose or my weight or things like that—not in the same way.\n\nIt’s just that scar feels so noticeable to me. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks in my own head, but when I’m at church or playing bridge, especially with my hands out on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about how other parts of your body look, beyond just the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I suppose everyone has little things they don’t love, especially at my age, but it isn’t like that for me.\n\nIt’s the wrist. That scar just catches my eye, and I know it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hands right there on the table—oh, and bridge, you know, people really do look at your hands because of the cards—and I keep feeling like their eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I’ve had arguments with family or anything, but I’ve pulled back from some things. Choir, for instance—I’ve skipped a few times, and I was supposed to help with a theater volunteer thing, but I didn’t go because I got so caught up trying to figure out what sleeve would cover my wrist, or whether people would notice.\n\nAnd my bookkeeping work too, actually. I’ve been late turning things in, which isn’t like me. I sit there meaning to work, but then I’m fussing with my wrist or thinking about whether it’ll show when I go out later. It sounds silly when I say it, but it takes up more time than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to avoid any important events or activities because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes… not big family things or anything like that, but I have skipped choir a few times, and there was a theater volunteer thing I was meant to help with that I didn’t go to. I just got so caught up with what I could wear so my wrist wouldn’t show, and then I felt embarrassed and stayed home.\n\nAnd even with my bookkeeping, I’ve been late turning things in, which really bothers me because I’m usually careful about that. I’ll mean to sit down and finish it, and then I’m thinking about my sleeve or whether the scar is visible… it’s ridiculous, but it can eat up the time.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to focus on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am, though I hate admitting that because I’ve always been very reliable with my bookkeeping. I’ll sit down to do the accounts, and then I notice my sleeve has ridden up, or I start thinking about whether the scar will show later if I go out, and suddenly I’ve lost all this time.\n\nI’ve turned things in late because of it, which isn’t like me. And it’s not just work—I’ve missed choir a few times, and that theater volunteer commitment, because I got so caught up trying to make sure my wrist wouldn’t be noticed. I know there are worse problems to have, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about the scar more about the way it looks to you or about what you think others might think when they see it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but… more what I imagine other people are seeing, maybe. I know, in my sensible mind, the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as awful or noticeable as it seems to me. But then I’ll be at church, or playing bridge, and I just get this feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like that’s the first thing they notice about me now, not my face or what I’m saying or anything. And I know people are probably busy thinking about their own things—at bridge half the time everyone’s worried about the bidding anyway—but I still feel like they’re looking.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, is it more the way it looks that bothers you, or is it what you think it means about you to other people?\n\nPatient: It’s… I suppose it’s the way it looks, but mostly what I imagine it says to other people when they see it. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—my sister would probably tell me nobody is studying my wrist at church, you know, people are looking at the hymn book or chatting about who brought what to coffee hour.\n\nBut when I’m there, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not that I’m there, not what I’m saying—just that. It makes me feel very exposed, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people seeing your scar, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you think it will lead people to judge you as a person?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more that it makes me look… noticeable in the wrong way. Not attractive or unattractive exactly, though I don’t like the look of it at all. I know the scar on my wrist probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I start wondering what they’re thinking, but it begins with how it looks, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about what people think of you if the scar on your wrist were to disappear?\n\nPatient: If it disappeared? I think… I think I wouldn’t be worrying in the same way, no. I mean, I know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or at bridge I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it.\n\nLike it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get all distracted—especially at bridge, which is silly because I should be counting cards, not worrying about my wrist. So if it weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t have that same thought in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about your appearance at all if you were completely alone and nobody could see you?\n\nPatient: If I were completely alone… I think it wouldn’t be as strong, maybe. It’s mostly when I imagine other people seeing it. Like at church, or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like that’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI know, logically, the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. My sister would say, “Nobody’s looking that closely,” and she’s probably right. But when I’m around people, it just feels very obvious. If no one could see me at all, I suppose I might not worry about it in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally satisfied with your body image, aside from the concern about the scar?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “satisfied,” exactly. I mean, at my age you get used to things changing, don’t you? I try not to fuss too much about it. My sister is always saying we earned our wrinkles, which is easy for her to say because she’s always had nicer skin than me—well, not nicer, just different, I suppose.\n\nBut aside from the scar… I don’t know. I don’t spend a great deal of time thinking about the rest of me, not like that. It’s really the wrist that seems to pull my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you less as a person if the scar on your wrist were not visible?\n\nPatient: Less as a person… I don’t know if I’d put it quite that way. I don’t think people at church are cruel, really—Mrs. Hanley still saves me a seat sometimes, unless her cousin comes, and then the whole pew arrangement changes, you know how that is.\n\nBut yes, I suppose if the scar weren’t visible, I wouldn’t keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. I know it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but at bridge especially I get this feeling it’s the first thing they notice now, before anything else about me. So maybe I’d feel less… judged, or less looked at, if they couldn’t see it."
